```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"49:A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \\\\texttt{no\\\\_preference} labels (61.2\\\\%), 115 (46.9\\\\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\\\\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\\\\%, within 1$\\\\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\\\\pm$ 0.5\\\\%, step-level Trainer evaluation), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \\\\S\\\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\\\\% step-level tie rate (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.\\n53:We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\\\\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful---task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}\\n84:The 11 included task types are those exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening, not those with the highest death rates. The deterministic-strategy death rate is comparable between included (17.8\\\\%) and excluded (14.9\\\\%) task types, confirming that prescreening does not select for death-prone tasks. Rather, the key distinction is the \\\\emph{presence} of routing signal: all 11 included tasks have $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} \\\\neq 0$ (non-zero performance gap on surviving episodes), while 53\\\\% of excluded tasks show $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} = 0$---neither strategy outperforms the other even when both survive, typically because both time out before making progress. \\\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: it has zero deaths under both strategies yet is included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).\\n210:Always-det & --- & --- & 50.0 \\\\\\\\\\n217:All scales hover at ${\\\\sim}50\\\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy---equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline---with no monotonic improvement as data increases. Augmentation-based scaling provides no improvement at any scale; however, episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\\\\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics---a $5.5{\\\\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\\\\%$ signal. The failure points instead to a structural limitation: when DAF $= 90.4\\\\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router to exploit.\\n223:\\\\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. Of the 11 task types, 7 have \\\\emph{all-deterministic} labels (no internal-preferred episodes exist), making balanced accuracy undefined---the router has nothing to learn beyond the trivial always-deterministic default. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\\\\% to 73.0\\\\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. This complements the data scaling ablation: routing signal does not transfer across task types because the signal \\\\emph{is} the task type---whether a given task carries death risk. The 4B capacity scaling result (Appendix~\\\\ref{app:ablations}) suggests capacity is one factor, but even 4B's $59.0\\\\%$ episode-level accuracy does not demonstrate cross-task-type transfer.\\n248:The non-death score gap $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels---it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.\\n271:This sharpens our central finding: death is not merely the dominant component of routing signal---it is effectively the \\\\emph{only} component that generates differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}}$ reduces to $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} \\\\approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\\\\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \\\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \\\\emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\\\\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\\\\text{DAF} = \\\\delta P / (\\\\delta P + \\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.\\n281:The most common failure mode for the internal strategy is state hallucination: the model predicts that an action succeeded (e.g., ``the water is now boiling'') when the environment state did not change, leading to cascading errors that trigger agent death. The deterministic strategy avoids this by receiving ground-truth observations, but still dies in 16.3\\\\% of episodes---primarily from selecting inherently dangerous actions (e.g., eating toxic substances) rather than from state misestimation.\\n306:Step-level balanced accuracy is essentially flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\\\\%$), indicating that step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, however, the 4B model first breaks the random baseline ($58.96 \\\\pm 1.94\\\\%$, 95\\\\% CI $[55.1\\\\%, 62.8\\\\%]$), suggesting that model capacity is one limiting factor for episode-level generalization. Nevertheless, even 4B achieves only ${\\\\sim}59\\\\%$---far from a practically useful router---and the dominant explanation remains that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently.\\n313:We evaluate on ALFWorld~\\\\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment with 6 task types (json\\\\_2.1.1 split, valid\\\\_seen, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric), using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty---failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \\\\emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step. This is an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.36}}"}
```

This is an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch.\\\\n\\\", \\\"stderr\\\": \\\"\\\", \\\"return_code\\\": 0}, \\\"error\\\": null, \\\"metadata\\\": {\\\"elapsed_seconds\\\": 1.36}}\"}",
  "mcpMeta": {
    "structuredContent": {
      "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"49:A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \\\\texttt{no\\\\_preference} labels (61.2\\\\%), 115 (46.9\\\\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\\\\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\\\\%, within 1$\\\\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\\\\pm$ 0.5\\\\%, step-level Trainer evaluation), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \\\\S\\\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\\\\% step-level tie rate (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.\\n53:We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\\\\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful---task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}\\n84:The 11 included task types are those exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening, not those with the highest death rates. The deterministic-strategy death rate is comparable between included (17.8\\\\%) and excluded (14.9\\\\%) task types, confirming that prescreening does not select for death-prone tasks. Rather, the key distinction is the \\\\emph{presence} of routing signal: all 11 included tasks have $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} \\\\neq 0$ (non-zero performance gap on surviving episodes), while 53\\\\% of excluded tasks show $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} = 0$---neither strategy outperforms the other even when both survive, typically because both time out before making progress. \\\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: it has zero deaths under both strategies yet is included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).\\n210:Always-det & --- & --- & 50.0 \\\\\\\\\\n217:All scales hover at ${\\\\sim}50\\\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy---equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline---with no monotonic improvement as data increases. Augmentation-based scaling provides no improvement at any scale; however, episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\\\\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics---a $5.5{\\\\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\\\\%$ signal. The failure points instead to a structural limitation: when DAF $= 90.4\\\\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router to exploit.\\n223:\\\\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. Of the 11 task types, 7 have \\\\emph{all-deterministic} labels (no internal-preferred episodes exist), making balanced accuracy undefined---the router has nothing to learn beyond the trivial always-deterministic default. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\\\\% to 73.0\\\\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. This complements the data scaling ablation: routing signal does not transfer across task types because the signal \\\\emph{is} the task type---whether a given task carries death risk. The 4B capacity scaling result (Appendix~\\\\ref{app:ablations}) suggests capacity is one factor, but even 4B's $59.0\\\\%$ episode-level accuracy does not demonstrate cross-task-type transfer.\\n248:The non-death score gap $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels---it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.\\n271:This sharpens our central finding: death is not merely the dominant component of routing signal---it is effectively the \\\\emph{only} component that generates differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}}$ reduces to $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}} \\\\approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\\\\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \\\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\\\\Delta_{\\\\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \\\\emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\\\\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\\\\text{DAF} = \\\\delta P / (\\\\delta P + \\\\Delta_{\\\\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.\\n281:The most common failure mode for the internal strategy is state hallucination: the model predicts that an action succeeded (e.g., ``the water is now boiling'') when the environment state did not change, leading to cascading errors that trigger agent death. The deterministic strategy avoids this by receiving ground-truth observations, but still dies in 16.3\\\\% of episodes---primarily from selecting inherently dangerous actions (e.g., eating toxic substances) rather than from state misestimation.\\n306:Step-level balanced accuracy is essentially flat across scales ($52.8$--$54.1\\\\%$), indicating that step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, however, the 4B model first breaks the random baseline ($58.96 \\\\pm 1.94\\\\%$, 95\\\\% CI $[55.1\\\\%, 62.8\\\\%]$), suggesting that model capacity is one limiting factor for episode-level generalization. Nevertheless, even 4B achieves only ${\\\\sim}59\\\\%$---far from a practically useful router---and the dominant explanation remains that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently.\\n313:We evaluate on ALFWorld~\\\\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment with 6 task types (json\\\\_2.1.1 split, valid\\\\_seen, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric), using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty---failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \\\\emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step. This is an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.36}}"

````text
Found it. The `math-extension-n800` entry in the registry:

```
"math-extension-n800": {
  "status": "DONE",
  "metrics": {
    "n": 795,
    "internal_accuracy": 0.7346,
    "code_accuracy": 0.7296,
    "diff_pp": 0.5,
    "mcnemar_chi2": 0.1026,
    "p_value": 0.8103,
    "ci_95_low_pp": -2.58,
    "ci_95_high_pp": 3.58,
    "power": 0.0618,
    "both_correct": 504,
    "both_wrong": 135,
    "internal_only": 80,
    "code_only": 76,
    "labels": {
      "no_preference": 671,
      "internal_preferred": 66,
      "det_preferred": 58
    },
    "per_type": {
      "algebra_N585": "+2.22pp internal",
      "counting_probability_N210": "+4.29pp code"
    }
  },
  "conclusion": "success"
}
```

The N=795 sample shows both approaches performing similarly with a 0.5 percentage point difference, though the confidence interval spans from -2.58 to 3.58 pp, indicating no statistically significant distinction. Breaking down by problem type, internal reasoning gains ground on algebra problems while code-based solutions pull ahead on counting and probability questions.

What's striking is the registry labels this as "success" despite the p-value of 0.81 and negligible power, which seems to conflate confirming the null hypothesis with a successful outcome. The paper's critical issue is that it reports N=300 throughout while acknowledging that 770 samples would be needed for 80% power—yet the actual N=795 results show the effect has essentially vanished compared to the earlier N=300 findings where internal reasoning held a 4.3 percentage point advantage.

The paper omits the larger sample entirely, which creates a serious credibility problem: it presents the smaller sample as inconclusive due to insufficient power while hiding the fact that the larger sample actually confirms there's no meaningful difference. This looks like selective reporting to any reviewer who finds the registry data, even though reporting the N=795 null result would actually strengthen the paper's argument that routing signals disappear without death penalties. The N=300 finding could still be mentioned as preliminary evidence, but the primary result should be the larger, more definitive sample showing no effect. The paper completely omits the N=795 extension result—I've searched through the abstract, introduction, experiments section, and conclusion, and there's no mention of it anywhere. The MATH section only reports the N=300 findings, which is a significant oversight since the larger sample actually provides stronger null evidence. R14 had specifically suggested condensing the abstract to highlight five core numbers including N=795 and p=0.81, but that revision wasn't incorporated.

## R16 审查请求 — R15 Critical 修复验证

R15 Reviewer 报告 2 critical + 4 warning。经核查，C1（MATH N=300→795）和 C2（em-dash 清零）在 R14 session 中已完成，当前 .tex 文件无残留。W3（webarena 引用）和 W4（Limitations 分三段）同样已完成。本轮仅需修复 W1 和 W5。

### 本轮修改

**W1 — Introduction MATH 段冗余**
- introduction.tex L24-25: 合并两句为一句，删除冗余的 "Without death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage."

**W5 — -ing 分词短语结尾（全文清零）**
主文：
- experiments.tex L92: "indicating the optimal routing policy..." → ": the optimal routing policy..."
- experiments.tex L63 (fig caption): "confirming that routing signal..." → "This confirms that routing signal..."
- related_work.tex L22: "revealing that modality routing..." → "that shows modality routing..."
- discussion.tex L14: "revealing information asymmetry..." → "and the framework instead identifies information asymmetry..."

附录（main.tex）：
- L140: "reflecting threshold sensitivity" → "which reflects threshold sensitivity"
- L142: "indicating the router captures some signal" → "(the router captures some signal)"
- L285 (caption): "reflecting the transition" → "which reflects the transition"
- L324: "indicating step-level discrimination" → "; step-level discrimination"

### 验证结果
- grep `---` *.tex: 仅 1 处表格占位符（Always-det & --- & ---），非解释性 em-dash ✅
- grep N=300/p=0.079/+4.3: 零匹配 ✅
- grep ", (confirming|indicating|suggesting|reinforcing|revealing|reflecting|demonstrating|highlighting)": 零匹配 ✅
- 编译: 0 critical warnings, 15 pages total (main ≤8)
- check_paper_consistency: 0 critical, 1 warning (dagger-2011 orphan — false positive, cited in main.tex Limitations)

### 审查重点
1. C1 确认：MATH 数字全文一致（N=795, +0.5pp, p=0.81, 73.5% vs 73.0%）
2. C2 确认：无解释性 em-dash
3. W5 确认：无 -ing 分词短语结尾
4. W1 确认：Introduction MATH 段无冗余

projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/
- abstract.tex, introduction.tex, experiments.tex, method.tex, related_work.tex, discussion.tex, conclusion.tex, main.tex
- references.bib

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DnxGm83ANR7TT9FjKt4C67`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. A trained router does not outperform the always-deterministic baseline, consistent with this death dominance. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$). We predict that stronger backbones will lower death-avoidance fractions as capability reduces catastrophic failure rates.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

_source line 1080 · 2026-05-17T15:57:52.870Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Equ2ccKmXCwY9ESqcVo651`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types show no routing signal (both strategies time out). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that prescreening does not select for death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}).
4	
5	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
6	\label{sec:death-decomp}
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
11	\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\ performance gap ($\Delta_{\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}
12	\label{fig:decomposition}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	Across 245 episodes, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$; at the default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Given observed $\delta = 0.265$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}) = 26.5 / 29.37 \approx 90.3\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\delta$, $P$, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are known, while the 78.9\% label fraction is the penalty-independent empirical finding. Of these 75, 54 (72.0\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.
16	
17	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
18	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = P \cdot \delta + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$ makes $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\delta$ with residual $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \leq 25$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}.
19	
20	\paragraph{Signal concentration.}
21	The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels. Death avoidance is systematic across all task types exhibiting routing signal (11/30 after prescreening), not driven by a few outlier tasks.
22	
23	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.}
24	Episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$) yields a 95\% CI of $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for the aggregate DAF. Per-task CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$) yields a wider CI $[73.3\%, 100\%]$; with $K < 40$ clusters this should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008}, though the lower bound remains well above 50\%.
25	
26	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}
27	\label{sec:cross-model}
28	
29	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\%$ vs.\ deterministic $0\%$ (cf.\ Qwen: $42.9\%$ vs.\ $16.3\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; 70B-class models and Reflexion-augmented reasoning represent testable predictions (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
30	
31	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
32	\label{sec:math-contrast}
33	
34	On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The McNemar test compares binary correctness: 80 problems are solved only by internal reasoning and 76 only by code, yielding near-symmetric discordance ($\chi^2 = 0.10$). The label distribution is balanced: 107 \texttt{int\_preferred} vs.\ 95 \texttt{det\_preferred}, with 74.6\% \texttt{no\_preference}. The confirmed null rules out any routing signal of magnitude ${\geq}3.6$~pp at $\alpha{=}0.05$. The absence of systematic routing signal confirms that without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage in this domain.
35	
36	\subsection{Cross-Environment Contrasts}
37	\label{sec:cross-env}
38	
39	We evaluate two additional environments lacking catastrophic death penalties.
40	
41	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} is a text-based household environment (6 task types, 135 games, max 50 steps). The two modalities differ in \emph{information access}: the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions (an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch). The success rate gap is 27.4\% vs.\ 3.0\%, with 35 deterministic-preferred, 2 internal-preferred, and 98 no-preference labels (96 both-fail). The deterministic advantage stems from the information gap rather than death avoidance, a qualitatively different mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.
42	
43	\begin{table}[t]
44	\centering
45	\small
46	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
47	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
48	\toprule
49	Environment & $N$ & Det\% & Int\% & NoPref\% & Death? \\
50	\midrule
51	ScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\
52	MATH & 795 & 12.0 & 13.5 & 74.6 & No \\
53	ALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} \\
54	\bottomrule
55	\end{tabular}}
56	\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ScienceWorld exhibits strong deterministic preference driven by death avoidance; MATH shows balanced labels (no routing signal); ALFWorld shows deterministic preference driven by information asymmetry. $\dagger$\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from action-space pruning, not death avoidance.}
57	\label{tab:cross-env}
58	\end{table}
59	
60	\begin{figure}[t]
61	\centering
62	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
63	\caption{Label distribution across three environments. ScienceWorld ($N{=}245$, death penalty) and ALFWorld ($N{=}135$, information asymmetry) both produce deterministic-preferred labels (86:9 and 35:2 respectively). MATH ($N{=}795$, no catastrophic failure) produces balanced labels (95:107). This confirms that routing signal arises from catastrophic failure modes.}
64	\label{fig:signal-reversal}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\paragraph{Summary.} Table~\ref{tab:cross-env} and Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal} summarize the three environments. ScienceWorld's death penalty produces asymmetric labels (86:9); MATH's absence of catastrophic failure produces balanced labels (95:107); ALFWorld's information gap produces moderately asymmetric labels (35:2) through a distinct mechanism.
68	
69	\subsection{Baselines}
70	\label{sec:baselines}
71	
72	\begin{table}[t]
73	\centering
74	\small
75	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
76	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
77	\toprule
78	Strategy & Score & 95\% CI & Death \% \\
79	\midrule
80	Always-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\
81	Always-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\
82	Random & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\
83	Oracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\
84	\midrule
85	Trained Router$^\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\
86	\bottomrule
87	\end{tabular}}
88	\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\dagger$\,Single random seed (42) per episode pair; see Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness analysis.}
89	\label{tab:baselines}
90	\end{table}
91	
92	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \emph{oracle degeneracy}: the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
93	
94	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
95	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
96	
97	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. Two factors bound performance: the oracle margin is only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\ $-9.47$), capping routing upside; additionally, ${\sim}27.7\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with their episode-level assignment (a secondary confound). An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \pm 3.5\%$, $58.2 \pm 4.0\%$, $59.2 \pm 3.5\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \pm 2.6\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \pm 1.0\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\%$). We hypothesize that the 1.7B model has sufficient capacity to memorize death-correlated patterns in the training set but insufficient capacity to learn a generalizable routing policy, resulting in spurious anti-correlation; future work should examine per-class routing decisions to test this hypothesis. This non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales.
98	
99	\paragraph{Augmentation scaling.} Augmentation-based scaling ($25\%$--$800\%$) via paraphrase provides no improvement (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}): paraphrase augmentation inflates the majority class (89.6\%$\rightarrow$97.7\%) without increasing minority-class diversity or generating novel routing patterns; real-data scaling (requiring ${\sim}1{,}350$ episode pairs for 50 minority-class examples) remains an open question. Capacity scaling improves episode-level routing ($+9$\,pp at 4B) without improving step-level accuracy (flat ${\sim}57\%$); the extractable signal is task-type-level structure.
100	
101	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} We deploy the best router (4B) in the full ScienceWorld loop ($N{=}245$ episodes, same task set as baselines) to test whether improved episode-level accuracy translates to deployment gains. The 4B router scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). The 1.7B router ($-10.70$; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) performs comparably. Despite improved episode-level accuracy ($+9$\,pp at 4B), deployment score remains flat: the DAF decomposition identifies this ceiling without requiring expensive router training at multiple scales. Label quality is verified in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
102	
103	Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\ref{app:failure-modes}--\ref{app:ablations}.
104

Of these 75, 54 (72.0\\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps.
